## Page 1


A Tb TYPE THEOREM
FOR SUPPRESSED KERNELS
MARINA FERNÀNDEZ-VILASECA∗
Universitat Autònoma de Barcelona (UAB)
Abstract
In this article, a non-homogeneous Tb type theorem for arbitrary dimensional Calderón-
Zygmund singular integral operators is proved. This is an extension of an analogous non-
homogeneous Tb theorem for the Cauchy transform, in the planar setting, due to Nazarov,
Treil and Volberg. The novelties of the present work are the change of dimension and the
fact that the operators to which the theorem applies are not necessarily antisymmetric.
The techniques used in the proof include, among others, suppressed kernels, decompositions
in L2(µ), where µ is a Radon measure in Rd, and a probabilistic argument resulting from
taking averages of the operators involved.

Introduction
A classical problem that has led to the development of many techniques in Analysis is Painlevé’s
problem on the metric or geometric characterization of removable sets for bounded analytic
functions. More precisely, given a compact set E ⊂C, we say that it is removable for bounded
analytic functions, or simply, removable, if for every open set Ω⊃E, every bounded and analytic
function on Ω\ E admits an analytic extension to the whole of Ω. For example, if E is a finite
collection of points, it is removable, but if we take E to be a disk, it is not.
A key tool in the description of removable sets is that of analytic capacity, which was introduced
by Ahlfors [Ahl47] and is defined as follows. For a compact set E ⊂C, we let its analytic capacity
be the number
γ(E) := sup |f′(∞)| = sup

lim
|z|→+∞z (f(z) −f(∞))
,
where the supremum is taken over all f ∈H(C \ E) with |f| ≤1 in C \ E. The connection
between analytic capacity and removability is described by a result, due to Ahlfors, which states
that a compact set E ⊂C is removable if and only if γ(E) = 0. Even though this is a positive
result in the characterization of removable sets, since it is a purely analytic characterization,
rather than a metric or geometric one, it fails to be the sought answer to Painlevé’s problem.
Another capacity, which turns out to have a better geometric interpretation, is the analytic
capacity γ+ (or capacity γ+), which is defined, for E ⊂C compact,
γ+(E) = sup µ(E),

that their Cauchy transform,
Cµ(x) =

is in L∞(C), with modulus bounded above by 1. One advantage of this new capacity is that it
has a precise description, due to Tolsa [Tol99], using previous ideas from Melnikov and Verdera
[MV95], in terms of curvature of measures, which is, indeed, a geometric notion, which was first
introduced by Melnikov [Mel95].
From this, we see that the missing piece to solve Painlevé’s problem is how γ and γ+ are related.
This was dealt with by Tolsa in [Tol03], in which he proved that there exists an absolute constant
A such that for any compact set E ⊂C,
γ+(E) ≤γ(E) ≤Aγ+(E),
which we write γ+ ≈γ, and we say that the capacities γ and γ+ are comparable.
A key
consequence of this result is that a compact set E ⊂C is non-removable if and only if it supports
a positive Radon measure that satisfies a certain growth condition and has finite curvature (more
precisely, see [Tol03, Theorem 1.2]). This description closes Painlevé’s problem.
A key ingredient in Tolsa’s proof of the comparability between the capacities γ and γ+ is a Tb
type theorem, which is originally due to Nazarov, Treil and Volberg [NTV02]. See Tolsa [Tol14,
Section 5] for a careful explanation of this result in the case of the Cauchy transform. The aim
of the present work is to extend this theorem to a broader class of operators. Specifically, in
this work it is shown that the reasoning can be adapted for higher dimension and that one can
drop the assumption that the kernel that we deal with is antisymmetric. We will comment on
the organization of the proof after a brief exposition on the topic of Tb theorems.
Broadly speaking, the notion of “a Tb type theorem” refers to a criterion for the boundedness
of a special type of integral operators, which is obtained via their action on a suitable bounded
test function, which is usually denoted by b. The operators that appear in this context are
called singular integral operators (SIOs). More precisely, we consider a function k, which we
call kernel, defined on Rd × Rd except, perhaps, on the diagonal {(x, y) ∈Rd × Rd : x = y}, and
we let
Tµ(x) =
Z
k(x, y) dµ(y),
where µ is a Radon measure in Rd, and for f ∈L1
loc(µ), we set Tµf(x) = T(fµ)(x). Of course,
the convergence of these integrals deserves some thought, which will be done in the first section
of this work, in which we will also introduce Calderón-Zygmund kernels, a particular case of
which is the kernel
1
x−y that appears in the definition of the Cauchy transform above. These
are the kernels that determine the operators appearing in Tb type theorems.
The family of theorems which we refer to as Tb type was developed as a tool to study the
L2(µ) boundedness of singular integral operators. For instance, the ones in David, Journé and
Semmes [DJS85] and in Christ [Chr90]. These theorems fall into the category known as classical
Calderón-Zygmund theory. The adjective is due to the fact that most of these theorems assume
that the underlying measure µ is doubling, which means that there is some constant c > 0 such
that for all x ∈supp(µ) and r > 0,
µ(B(x, 2r)) ≤c µ(B(x, r)).
Originally, it was believed that reasonable results for Calderón-Zygmund singular integral oper-
ators could only be obtained in spaces for which the underlying measure satisfies the condition
above. These kinds of spaces are called homogeneous. However, when working with analytic
3


## Page 4


capacity, it is not uncommon to find a situation where the doubling assumption above fails.
For instance, if the measure µ is the Hausdorff 1-dimensional measure restricted to a compact
set. Nevertheless, in this type of context, an alternative and natural assumption is that µ has
polynomial growth of degree n ≤d, meaning that for r > 0 and x ∈Rd,
µ(B(x, r)) ≤c0 rn.
(1)
For example, in Tolsa [Tol99], a non-doubling version for the T1 theorem, that is, for the simpler
case that b = 1, for the Cauchy transform is proved.
The Tb theorem in the present work falls into the non-homogeneous setting. It will concern a
finite Radon measure µ without any doubling assumption. Moreover, the polynomial growth
condition (1) will not necessarily be true for all the balls B(x, r).
Instead, we will allow a
controlled family of balls to have bigger measure. In this sense, we will not obtain that our
singular integral operator T is bounded in L2(µ), but rather in L2(µ⌊G), where the µ-measure
of G is not too small compared to that of supp(µ), and µ⌊G will have the desired polynomial
growth. Moreover, since the antisymmetric assumption is removed, the theorem deals with two
bounded functions b1, b2, satisfying that
Z
T∗(b1µ) dµ ≤c∗∥µ∥,
Z
T ∗
∗(b2µ) dµ ≤c∗∥µ∥,
where the integrals are taken in the complementary of a distinguished set. The complete details
about the hypothesis and conclusions can be found in the precise statement, which is Theorem
2.1 from Section 2.
Let us remark that the proof of the Tb theorem is going to be highly technical, as is the case in
[NTV02] and even in the simpler case [Tol14]. This is the reason why we have decided to split
the proof into several sections, which we outline now.
As we mentioned above, the first section is an introduction to the theory of Calderón-Zygmund
kernels and singular integral operators. We give the basic definitions of the concepts and state-
ments of results that will be key in the rest of the thesis. Some are very well-known and are
the same in both the classical and the non-homogeneous setting, such as the dyadic Hardy-
Littlewood differentiation theorem, but not all are valid in both cases. For instance, we will
need a Calderón-Zygmund decomposition tailored to the non-homogeneous case. The proof of
most of the results from this preliminary section can be found in [Tol14, Section 2].
The second section contains the complete statement of the Tb theorem that we prove, which is
Theorem 2.1. After stating it, the following sections are devoted to establishing the necessary
tools for the rather long proof.
We define what we call an exceptional set, which, morally,
controls the portion of supp(µ) where the maximal operator associated to T is big. Afterwards,
we consider, for a Lipschitz function Θ : Rd →[0, +∞), an appropriate modification of the
Calderón-Zygmund kernel that defines T, which we call ekΘ, the “suppressed” version of k, with
associated operator KΘ. This notion was introduced by Nazarov, Treil and Volberg [NTV] and
is helpful for overcoming the difficulties associated with the non-homogeneous setting.
Still in the second section, we introduce the essential concepts related to dyadic lattices. Instead
of fixing one lattice, we will work with its translates, and for each of these we are going to classify
its cubes according to their position with respect to some of the sets that are singled out in the
statement of our Tb theorem. Later, we will see that this classification, along with a family
of operators indexed by the cubes from the lattice, yields a decomposition in L2(µ), which is
Lemma 2.21, and will be central in the rest of the proof.
In the last section of the second section, we introduce the notion of good and bad cubes, from
one dyadic lattice with respect to another. The third section is devoted to proving a lemma
4


## Page 5


concerning the action of KΘ on good functions, which are essentially the ones in which the
L2(µ) decomposition only sees the good cubes. The proof of this lemma is long and technical,
and consists of using different strategies depending the type of cubes appearing in the L2(µ)
decomposition.
The fourth section is quite brief and deals with a Cotlar type theorem for a certain type of
Calderón-Zygmund operators. Its proof will be independent of the classifications of dyadic cubes
mentioned above and it will use the non-doubling Calderón-Zygmund decomposition stated in
the first section.
The last section is devoted to concluding the proof of the Tb theorem through a probabilistic
argument. It consists of showing that the probability that of being in a bad situation, in the
sense of bad cubes and functions, can be made arbitrarily close to zero. Moreover, here we define
the set G on which our original operator T is going to be bounded in L2.
One of the motivations for introducing the non-homogeneous Tb theorem was the proof of the
comparability between the capacities γ and γ+. Of course, this was a result for the planar case,
so it did not need the generalization that we prove in this work. Thus, it is reasonable to ask
whether the additional work that we have done to prove the theorem in arbitrary dimension has
a similar justification. The answer is affirmative and it concerns a reformulation of the problem
of removability.
This reformulation refers to changing the type of functions that we study, switching from analytic
functions to Lipschitz harmonic functions. So far, we have considered functions defined on Ω\E,
where Ω⊂C is open and E is compact. Then, we look for conditions on the set E that ensure
that for f bounded and analytic in Ω\E, we actually have that ∂f(z) = 0 for all z ∈Ω. We can
write this problem in terms of only real functions, as follows. Instead of holomorphic functions,
we consider harmonic functions: u : Ω\ E →R such that ∆u = 0. Equivalently, such that
∂∂u = 0. We can call f = ∂u, and we see that u is harmonic if and only if ∂u is analytic.
Using this, one sees that if γ(E) = 0, then E is removable for Lipschitz harmonic functions.
The converse implication is also true, but it requires using the fact that γ ≈γ+.
In this context, it is natural to look for a Lipschitz harmonic capacity of the set E, a quantity
that, similarly to analytic capacity, will vanish if and only if E is removable for Lipschitz
harmonic functions. By our previous comment, for E ⊂R2, this capacity vanishes if and only if
γ(E) = 0. Now, note that this new capacity can be defined in Rd, for d ≥2, that is, it extends
the notion of analytic capacity to a higher dimensional setting. Since the planar Tb theorem was
useful for studying analytic capacity, it seems reasonable to think that a d-dimensional analogue
of this theorem would be a useful tool to study Lipschitz harmonic capacity. This is indeed the
case, for more details, see Section 2 of Volberg’s book [Vol03] or [NTV14].
As a final remark, after completing the present work, I was informed by my advisor that Andrea
Merlo, Mihalis Mourgoglou, Carmelo Puliatti [MMP] notified him that, simultaneously and
independently of my work, they have proved a very general local Tb theorem, from which one
could derive the Tb theorem explained in this thesis.
Acknowledgement. I would like to thank Dr. Xavier Tolsa for supervising my Master’s thesis,
for suggesting the topic, and for his guidance and support throughout the development of this
work.

1
Preliminaries
In this section we introduce the basic notions related to the theory of singular integral operators
in Rd. We will discuss Calderón-Zygmund kernels, their adjoints and their maximal versions.
We will also introduce some basic aspects of non-doubling Calderón-Zygmund theory and dyadic
lattices, which are a key element in the main theorem of this thesis.
1.1
Calderón-Zygmund kernels and singular integral operators

dimensional Calderón-Zygmund kernel if there exist constants c > 0 and 0 < η ≤1, such
that the following inequalities hold for all x, y ∈Rd, x ̸= y,

1.2
The Calderón-Zygmund decomposition
As mentioned in the Introduction, the Tb theorem in the present work belongs to the ambit of
non-homogeneous Calderón-Zygmund theory, that is, we work with measures µ in Rd such that
the doubling condition
µ(B(x, 2r)) ≤c µ(B(x, r)),
for any x ∈Rd, r > 0,
with a constant c, independent of the balls, is not necessarily true for all balls B(x, r) or cubes.
This is why we introduce, the following notion. For α, β > 1, we say that a cube Q ⊂Rd is
(α, β)-doubling if
µ(αQ) ≤βµ(Q),
where αQ denotes the cube concentric with Q with diameter α diam(Q). We claim that, although
µ may not satisfy any growth condition like the one above, there are a lot of small doubling
cubes. For a proof, see [Tol14, Lemma 2.8].
Lemma 1.5. Let β > αd. If µ is a Radon measure in Rd, then for µ-a.e. x ∈Rd, there exists a
sequence of (α, β)-doubling cubes {Qk} centered at the point x with ℓ(Qk) →0 as k →+∞.
A key tool in many results concerning SIOs is the following lemma, called the Calderón-
Zygmund decomposition. Morally, it says that if we consider a Radon measure µ on Rd,
then for any finite measure ν with compact support, we can split it into a “good” part, where it is
controlled by the ambient measure µ and a “bad” part, in which |ν| is bigger than µ. Moreover,
the latter is a union of cubes with finite overlap. For a proof, see [Tol14, Lemma 2.14].
Lemma 1.6. Let µ be a Radon measure on Rd. For every ν ∈M(Rd) with compact support
and every λ > 2d+1 ∥ν∥

1.3
The Hardy-Littlewood maximal operator
Let us introduce briefly an operator which is very well known in Harmonic Analysis: for a
Radon measure µ in Rd, the centered maximal Hardy-Littlewood operator Mµ applied
to a complex Radon measure ν is defined as
Mµν(x) = sup
r>0

loc(µ), we have Mµf = Mµ(fµ). A classical result, which can be proved using the
covering theorem for bounded subsets of Rd, due to Besicovitch, is the following.
Theorem 1.8. Let µ be a Radon measure in Rd. The centered maximal Hardy-Littlewood
operator Mµ is bounded from M(Rd) to L1,∞(µ) and in Lp(µ), for 1 < p ≤∞.
For our purposes, this theorem will be useful because it will enable us to relate boundedness of
two integral operators whenever their difference is controlled by the maximal Hardy-Littlewood
operator.
Another result concerning this maximal operator, which we will use in Section 4, is Cotlar’s
inequality, which we state next.
Theorem 1.9. Let µ be a positive Radon measure on Rd and let T be an n-dimensional SIO.
Let 0 < s ≤1, and 0 < δ ≤ε. Suppose that for some fixed x ∈Rd,
µ(B(x, r)) ≤c0rn

Preliminaries
Note that in the theorem above we impose no doubling conditions on the measure µ. Hence,
this theorem is valid for the non-homogeneous setting, which will be the situation in the Tb
theorem that we will prove. This result is due to Nazarov, Treil and Volberg [NTV98], although
the form in which we have stated it here is from Tolsa [Tol98].

for any r > 0, then MRν(x) ≤c0Mµν(x), and so MR is bounded in Lp(µ) for 1 < p ≤∞and
from M(Rd) to L1,∞(µ).

A terminology that is often used when working with dyadic lattices and cubes, related to the
second property, is the following. Suppose that we have some family I ⊂D of not necessarily
disjoint dyadic cubes. We say that a cube in this family is maximal if it is not contained in any
other cube from the family. We denote by Imax ⊆I the subfamily of the maximal cubes. The
advantage of considering the maximal family is that the cubes that form it are pairwise disjoint.

A Tb type theorem
Below we state the main theorem of the present work. As stated in the Introduction, this is an
extension of an analogous result by Nazarov, Treil and Volberg [NTV02]. The theorem concerns
a SIO associated to, in the sense of Definition 1.2, an n-dimensional Calderón-Zygmund kernel
k(·, ·), defined on Rd×Rd\{(x, y) ∈R2×Rd : x = y}, with associated constants CCZ > 0, which,
for commodity, will be denoted also by c, which will absorb other constants, and 0 < η ≤1 (in
the sense of Definition 1.1). Let us fix these constants throughout the rest of the article.

Before moving on to the proof, let us remark that it will be highly technical and quite long.
This is why it has been divided in three sections. In the present one, we introduce some objects,
which will be the right tools to prove Theorem 2.1, and we describe and prove their useful
properties. In Section 3, we prove a key lemma concerning one of these objects. Section 4 is
brief and consists in proving a theorem that is analogous to one of the results in the section
containing the preliminaries. Lastly, Section 5 combines all the work from the previous ones to
conclude the proof of Theorem 2.1 via a probabilistic argument.

The suppressed operators
As in [NTV] and [Tol14], we are going to consider a suppressed version of our kernel k(·, ·).
That is, we are going to modify it in such a way that we obtain a new kernel with properties
that make it easier to work with, while keeping it similar enough to the original one so that
it enables us to deduce properties which, without the modification, would be much harder to
prove.

(a) Every ball Br of radius r such that µ(Br) > c0rn is contained in T

The behavior of these balls motivates the following definition.
Definition 2.14. Let x ∈F. We say that a ball B(x, r) is non-n-Ahlfors if
µ(B(x, r)) > c0rn.
In addition, we define the n-Ahlfors radius of the point x as
R(x) = sup{r > 0 : µ(B(x, r)) > c0rn}
= sup{r > 0 : B(x, r) is a non-n-Ahlfors ball }.
If there does not exist any r > 0 such that µ(B(x, r)) > c0rn, we simply set R(x) = 0.
Note that for any w ∈Rd, all non-n-Ahlfors balls and the balls B(x, e1(x)), B(x, e2(x)), defined
in (16) and (17), are contained in HD(w) ∪S. This observation will be essential in proving the
following lemma.
Lemma 2.15. Let w ∈Rd and let Θ: Rd →[0, ∞) be a 1-Lipschitz function such that
Θ(x) ≥dist(x, Rd \ (HD(w) ∪S)),
for all x ∈Rd. Then, KΘ,∗ν1(x) ≤c4 and K∗
Θ,∗ν2(x) ≤c4 for all x ∈F, with c4 depending on
c0, cb, c∗, and δ0.
Proof. Let x ∈F and call r0 = max(R(x), e1(x), e2(x)). We claim that Θ(x) ≥r0.
Obviously, if x ̸∈HD(w) ∪S, r0 = R(x) = e1(x) = e2(x) = 0, so the inequality holds trivially. If
x ∈HD(w) ∪S, we can see that Θ(x) ≥R(x) and Θ(x) ≥max(e1(x), e2(x)) separately.
First, we show that Θ(x) ≥R(x).
Indeed, if R(x) = 0, it is clear.
If R(x) > 0, for any
0 < r < R(x), by definition of supremum there is some r < r′ ≤R(x) such that B(x, r′) is
non-n-Ahlfors, so B(x, r′) ⊂HD(w). Hence,
Θ(x) ≥dist(x, Rd \ (HD(w) ∪S)) ≥r′ > r,
for all
r < R(x),
from which we deduce that Θ(x) ≥R(x).
Now, to prove that Θ(x) ≥max(e1(x), e2(x)), we will only show the details of the proof of
Θ(x) ≥e1(x). The other inequality is proved analogously.
If x ̸∈S1
0, we have that e1(x) = 0 and so the inequality is clear. If x ∈S0, then x ∈B(x, e1(x)) ⊂
S, so
dist(x, Rd \ (HD(w) ∪S)) ≥dist(x, Rd \ (HD(w) ∪S1)) ≥e1(x),
and in this case the inequality is also satisfied. Analogously, Θ(x) ≥e2(x).
We can apply the last part of Lemma 2.13 with this choice of r0. Indeed, since r0 ≥R(x), for
r ≥r0 we have that µ(B(x, r)) ≤c0rn. Also, if ε ≥r0 ≥max(e1(x), e2(x)), by definition of

ε ν2(x)| ≤α. This allows us to deduce that for all ε > 0,

2.3
Dyadic lattices and the martingale decomposition
2.3.1
Random dyadic lattices
As we will see in the end of the proof of Theorem 2.1, a probabilistic argument will play a key
role in showing the L2(µ⌊G) boundedness of T. The motivation for introducing randomness is
that we are going to relate boundedness of our original operators with that of other operators
arising from taking averages (or, in probabilistic terms, expectations) over the points of a set
Ω⊂Rd with which we translate the usual dyadic lattice. In this section, we define the set Ω.

2.3.2
Transit and terminal cubes
Recall that in the first section we have singled out an exceptional set with the intention of
controlling the maximal functions T∗ν1 and T ∗
∗ν2. This will not be the only instance of the use
of such a technique in the proof of Theorem 2.1. In the present section, we are going to classify
the dyadic cubes of any given lattice, D, taking into account whether or not they are contained
in the special sets HD, T 1
D or T 2
D. We will see that, on the one hand, the cubes that are not
contained inside these sets have some desirable properties regarding the growth of the µ-measure
of their dilates and their side lengths. On the other hand, by considering the maximal cubes
25


## Page 26


2
A Tb type theorem
contained inside the special sets, we will obtain a disjoint family of cubes with side lengths
comparable to those of the better-behaved ones. We give this classification now.
Fix w ∈Ωand consider the dyadic lattice D ≡D(w). Let Q ∈D be contained in Q0
D = Q0(w)
with µ(Q) > 0. We say that the cube Q is
• terminal of the first (resp. second) kind if 2Q ⊂HD or if Q ⊂T 1
D (resp. 2Q ⊂HD or
Q ⊂T 2
D),
• transit of the first (resp. second) kind if it is not terminal of the first (resp. second)
kind.
We denote the set of cubes that are terminal of the first kind by Dterm,1, the set of those that
are terminal of the second kind by Dterm,2 and Dterm = Dterm,1 ∪Dterm,2 and analogously with
Dtr,1 and Dtr,2 for transit cubes.
Note that we assume all transit and terminal (of the first and second kind) cubes to be contained
in Q0
D. This is because if a cube (or part of it) is outside Q0

µ-measure (or the only contribution comes from inside Q0
D).

2.3.3
The martingale decomposition
So far, our classification of dyadic cubes of a given lattice has only given us information about
the measure µ on the dilates of the cubes and the relation between the µ-measure and the νi-
measure of said cubes. However, the distinction between transit and terminal cubes has much
more to offer. Indeed, in the following pages, we are going to define for each transit cube an
operator on L2(µ) and with a little work we will be able to express any function from this space
as a sum of these operators acting on our function. This is a standard technique in Harmonic

2.4
Good and bad cubes and functions
In the preceding section we have seen that the transit cubes, which enjoy some desirable proper-
ties with respect to the measure µ, can be used to decompose any function f ∈L2(µ). The next
step to study L2 boundedness of our operator T will be to use this decomposition when working
with the suppressed operators KΘ. However, the distinction between transit and terminal cubes
will not be enough, and we will need to further classify the transit cubes.
More precisely, we are now going to define what it means for a dyadic cube Q ∈Dtr,i(w1) to be
bad (or good) with respect to another dyadic lattice D(w2), with w1, w2 ∈Ω. The purpose of
doing so is that, using the decomposition in (44), we will be able to write f ∈L2(µ) as
f = fgood(w1, w2) + fbad(w1, w2)

dist(Q, R) = dist(Q, ∂R) ≤ℓ(Q)γℓ(R)1−γ.
Moreover, since Q is good, there does not exist any eR ∈D2 such that
dist(Q, ∂eR) ≤ℓ(Q)γℓ( eR)1−γ
and
ℓ( eR) ≥2mℓ(Q).
Hence, since R satisfies the first condition, it must be that ℓ(R) < 2mℓ(Q), that is, 2−mℓ(R) <
ℓ(Q), which is the first inequality in our claim. Regarding the second inequality, since m ≥1,
we have that ℓ(Q) ≤ℓ(R) < 2mℓ(R). Assume now that ℓ(Q) > ℓ(R). Then,
dist(Q, R) = dist(R, ∂Q) ≤ℓ(R)γℓ(Q)1−γ.
Since R is good, arguing analogously as before we find that ℓ(Q) < 2mℓ(R) and since m ≥1,
2−mℓ(R) < ℓ(R) < ℓ(Q).

Since the last bound is symmetric in Q and R (note that here it does not matter whether the

Before moving on to the next estimates, let us make a final remark about how we bounded the
sum S1. The fact that we were dealing only with transit cubes (of either kind) was crucial.
Indeed, it allowed us to use Lemma 2.17 and bound for every transit cube S and λ ≥1,
µ(λS) ≤c0ℓ(λS)n, which would not have been possible if there had been terminal cubes, for
which we do not have such a type of control, in the martingale decomposition given by Lemma
2.21.

5
A probabilistic argument
5.1
Low probability of bad cubes and functions
Let us remark that, up until now, all our efforts have been aimed towards estimating the behavior
of the suppressed operators KΘ when they act on good functions. Recall that our motivation for
obtaining such estimates, as mentioned in the beginning of Section 2.4, was that the probability
that a transit cube was bad with respect to a fixed dyadic lattice could be made arbitrarily
small. The aim of this section is to, first, give a more precise statement of such claim and,
afterwards, prove it.
Remember that for a transit cube Q ∈Dtr,1
1
, we say that it is bad with respect to the dyadic
lattice D2 if either
(a) there exists a cube R ∈D2 such that dist(Q, ∂R) ≤ℓ(Q)γℓ(R)1−γ and 2mℓ(Q) ≤ℓ(R) ≤
2N (where m ≥1 is some integer that we will fix below), or

Lastly, we deal with the sum A4, in which all the cubes that appear are transit. Unfortunately,
the same technique as in the two preceding lemmas fails, because the family of transit cubes
whose parents are also transit is not pairwise disjoint, unlike in the case where the children are
terminal and the parents are transit. To get an appropriate bound in this case, we will need to
separate each summand even further. We do this in the following lemma.

5.5
The final step
In this section, we are going to complete the proof of Theorem 2.1. The main ingredient in
this final step will be to show that the operator eK, whose kernel was the average of the kernels
ekΨw, with respect to P Ω2, is bounded in L2(µ). The key tools to achieve this will be, of course,
our result for good functions, Lemma 3.1, combined with the probabilistic estimates from the
preceding section.
